Ladder rungs write `data-ans`/`data-ansval` rather than opt()’s `data-set`, because on the convenor surface `data-set` already means *set the delegation* and one attribute must not mean two things on one page; each surface’s handler decides where answers land (the ceremony’s S, the convenor’s S.myAns). Postponing the close never lowers the bar (Ed, 2026-08-18; SPEC §4.3): the threshold keeps its current value and rises more slowly to the same ceiling over the new remainder. **Choosing and committing are two acts** (Ed, 363a, 2026-08-18): touching a consent control fills a *provisional* value — which is what wakes the ✓ — and the ✓ commits it; only committed answers count anywhere (the tally, the gates, the resolution), because a blind question's answers feed a count everyone sees and resolve to maxima, so a stray click while exploring the ladder must not be an answer. Motion consent takes the same gesture.
- `watch-half` — what a card grows underneath its own body once the answer is somebody else's (Ed, 2026-08-18: *the roster's cards are visible to the admin (with completion bar); when the roster's cards are complete they have the roster statistics on them*). While the question runs it can say only **how many have answered**, because any of the values or a running maximum would let the room read itself before it had finished, which is the whole reason the ceremony is blind; once it closes there is nothing left to anchor, so it carries the `distribution-strip` and what the document took. The same half on both surfaces, since neither the convenor nor a member who has answered may see more of a blind question than the other.
  - **Surface copy audit rules** (Ed's pass of 2026-08-18: *check the cards make sense, don't have unnecessary/wrong text, fit the design system*). What it caught, kept as rules: **no spec references in surface copy** (§-numbers cite a document members never see — say the rule, not its address); **no project-speak** (“v1 defaults” became “the standard rate”); **no engine jargon** on cards (“rolling log hash” left the chamber card); **the rename reaches the option labels** (“Roster only” → “Members only” in five places); **a shared body must not hard-code one caller's frame** (the quorum body said “asked as a count” whatever the convenor chose, and offered a count slider against a share question — it now takes the form); **read-only copy must survive the spec it summarises** (“fixed for the life of the document” predated motions and was false — the lockline now says what changing a setting actually takes, by kind); and **raw values are not copy** (the ⏰ answer line printed an unformatted datetime).
- **Task titles are Title Case** (Ed, 2026-08-18: *these tasks should be named in title case*), and the bare nouns drop their article — **Title**, **Link**, **Starting Text** (his renames, same day). **A title says what kind of answer it wants** (Ed, 2026-08-18: *whatever seems easiest to understand, I don't think they have to be consistent necessarily as long as they're clear — can you tell what the type of the answer is by the title?*). Which is a better rule than consistency, and it is the rule the two titles Ed wrote himself both follow: 🎩 *Is the convenor a member?* wants yes or no, 🪙 *How many ✏️s do members start with?* wants a number. Four titles named a topic without saying what it wanted back and changed accordingly — 👤 *When is a proposer named?*, ✍️ *Who may sign a proposal?*, 👁️ *When are judgments revealed?*, 🤖 *Is there a machine member?*. **Quorum** and **Approval threshold** stay nouns: both are terms of art the room will use all session and both plainly want a number. The rest are possessives or objects whose type is obvious. Open: whether a card should be titled differently once it is settled, since a rule at the head of a constitution reads better as *Approval threshold, 66%* than as a question (Q331).
- **One card per decision** (Ed, 2026-08-18: *go through every decision each role needs to make and turn it into a queue-card. Break them down as much as you can — picture and name separate! — admin choosing if they're a participant — etc*). Nineteen of them, and (coincidentally) nineteen on a member's screen too. The test is whether somebody could answer it **differently and on its own**, and both of Ed's examples are the same fault: two questions of *different kinds* bundled because they happened to be asked at the same moment. So a picture and a name are two cards (🙂, 📛); the roster and whether the convenor is in it were two (🪪, and a 🎩 card since re-folded into it as the pre-checked row); the economy is three (🪙 the grant, 💧 the drip, 🎲 the stake); the window is two (📅 whether it ends, ⏰ when). What is **not** split is a parameter and its own value — *rising, 55% → 78%* is one thought, and halving it would be splitting a sentence rather than a decision. The same test cuts the other way twice (Ed, 2026-08-18), which is what makes it a test rather than a direction of travel: ⏰ **when it ends** swallowed 📅 *whether it ends*, because **never** is one of the answers to *when* and nobody can answer the two differently; and 🎲 **the stake** stopped being a card at all, because it is a flat 1 in the mechanism (§13) and a card for it was offering a decision nobody has. Nineteen became seventeen.
  - `identity-cards` — ✋ *your name* (✋ since Ed, 2026-08-18: 📛 *is not very familiar to westerners*) and 🖼️ *your picture*, two cards since Ed, 2026-08-18. **Not authorship** (SPEC §9.0c): a name is how you appear **in the room** — the roster, the presence row, beside your own wallet — where authorship is whether a name is attached to a **proposal**, which is sealed by default. A document showing fourteen named people and not one named candidate is the ordinary case. It retires half of `me`'s *initials, not a photograph*: initials are now the default rather than the rule. *No invented photographs in a mockup* — the picker's first offer was a ground for your initials or a drawn mark, because a face here would be the one piece of fiction that reads as a claim about a real person — was **retired 2026-08-23 (Q734)**: it is a good rule about *fixtures* and had already stopped being one about *controls* when the uploader landed, and by then the grounds and the marks were two mockup devices surviving as stored formats in a card that had two real answers in it. A picture is now an emoji, an uploaded image, or none, and *none* is the initials — not a third thing to pick but what the room shows when you have given nothing, which is why the card says so in a sentence instead of offering it in a row. `c0`–`c5` and `m0`–`m2` are refused at the server rather than merely un-offered (Ed, same day: *we're still in alpha, you can mostly ignore backwards compatibility, there are no real documents yet*). **Or an emoji face** (Ed, 2026-08-19): the picker's own rows are Ed's emoji faces (`FACE_EMOJI` in setup.js, stored as `'e'+emoji`), generic by design so they claim nothing about a real person; the applicant's 🖼️ card carries the same row. **With a skin-tone selector since Ed's ask of 2026-08-19** (`FACE_TONES`/`faceToneRow`/`faceToned` in setup.js): six ✋ swatches, the Fitzpatrick modifier inserted after the *first code point* so ZWJ sequences tone correctly (👩🏽‍🦰); the selector is picker state, never stored — what is stored is the final toned emoji, so nobody's face depends on where the selector sits. And **an emoji is a glyph, not a disc** (Q732/Q735, Ed 2026-08-23: they render *very small and right aligned*, and should be *sized like the text around them and replace the circle that images use*). The first attempt at this was a specificity fix — `span.av.emoji` at (0,2,1), meant to beat every context's initials tuning — and it lost, silently, because every contextual avatar rule is written with three classes: `.cpv.founded .av` re-sized the emoji to **7px**, the value chosen for two initial letters, and `#assembly .seat .av` to 11px. The *right aligned* half was a second bug wearing the same coat: the base `.av` is `inline-grid; place-items: center`, `.cpv.founded .av` overrides `display` to `inline-flex`, where `place-items`' justify half means nothing — so with no `justify-content` the glyph fell to `flex-start` inside a 20px disc with `overflow: hidden`, crushed against an edge and clipped. It was miscentring the **initials** too, which is what makes it worth writing down: a rule that quietly drops half of what it inherits is invisible until something with a different aspect ratio stands in it. The fix is structural rather than another round of specificity — `avHtml`'s emoji branch emits `.emojiface`, which is not an `.av` at all, so no rule that sizes a disc can reach it, and one declaration (`font-size: 1em`) is right at every site at once. Two places state a size instead, because there is no text around them to take one from: the navbar `me` (whose 26px sets the topbar's height) and a seat in the assembly ring. **Any emoji may be a face — except the furniture, and except somebody else's** (Ed, 2026-08-19, twice): the picker's “Or any emoji” input takes one pictographic grapheme; `RESERVED_EMOJI` (setup.js) is a scan of the surface files for every glyph the vocabulary uses, minus the offered faces — a member whose face is ✏️ would turn every wallet into a possible mention of them — with tones and variation selectors stripped before the test (✋🏽 is as reserved as ✋); and **one emoji, one member, first come first served**: the takenness test is a page-installed hook (`setFaceTaken`, the wirePicDrop pattern), taken faces render disabled with “X got there first”, exact-match on the stored string so 👩🏻 and 👩🏽 are both claimable. Sizing: `.emojiface` takes `1em`, so it is 16px on the Founded line and 13px in a member row — measured, not asserted, by `design/tools/pic-measure.mjs`, which walks a founding to a document with two emoji faces in it and reports each glyph's computed size against its own parent's. `.emojiface.big` is 38px, for the picker grid and the *Currently* preview. **And the emoji path is fixtured now**: every picture in every fixture had been the empty string, which is why no probe, walk or golden could ever have caught the 7px face — one member of the founding cast wears 🦉, and the setup-probe opens 🖼️ and chooses one, which it had never done. **Avatars align by box, not by content baseline** (Ed's QA, same day, on the Founded line): inline-flex/grid boxes derive their baseline from their content, so a photo, initials and a drawn mark each sat differently beside the same text — the base `.av` takes `vertical-align: middle`, which is content-independent, and the founded avatar a 2px optical nudge. They sit in the **participants'** pile rather than the convenor's, on the reading that closes Q318: a name is a participant's own setting, and the two piles are *the document's settings the convenor holds* and *everything the participants hold, collectively or individually*.
  - `clerk` — a convenor who is not a drafter (Ed, 2026-08-17: *they should be able to decide whether they’re a drafter or just the clerk*). **Since Ed's QA of 2026-08-19 the hat is a card again** — 🎩 *Is the Founder a member?*, two radios (*Member — drafting too* / *Clerk — not drafting*) with what each role means under it, 🗑️✒️ as its commit row, locked once the document begins (§9.6a); its tab stands **in a tab stack with 🪪** on the Members paragraph, and the founder's row in the invitations list wears a plain chip like anybody's. The 2026-08-18 story follows for the reasoning it keeps: the hat question was **a pre-checked row in the membership card**, not a card: *is the convenor a member* is just whether the convenor invites themselves, so it is asked where the roster lives — their own row, first in the 🪪 card’s clause, ticked by default, with the toggle flipping member ↔ clerk. A clerk’s name and picture are **optional** (an anonymous convenor is ordinary; they simply set neither). After the start their membership is ordinary membership — it lapses and leaves like anybody’s — and the right to re-tick themselves dies with the start (SPEC §9.6a). This re-merges a pair the decomposition had split, and the one-card test explains why the merge is right: *whom do you invite* and *do you invite yourself* are answered with one gesture on one list, where the old 🎩 card asked you to answer your own row somewhere other than the list it is a row of. Eighteen cards. Convenor is a hat worn over a role rather than a role itself, so it is a card of its own — and since Ed, 2026-08-18 the card is the plain question **“Is the convenor a member?”**, answered **Yes / No**. *Your part in it* named a topic, and a topic has to be read before you know what is being asked; a question is answerable from its title. It is also the one card on the convenor’s surface that somebody else reads, so the third person is the point rather than a stiffness: the same words work on both screens, where *your part* only ever worked on one. The two roles have not gone — they moved into the line under each answer, which is where the wallet, the judgments and the place in quorum are actually explained. A clerk has no wallet, casts no judgments, counts towards no quorum and answers nothing at the ceremony. It is also why the delegation option is worded *you among them, if you are on the roster*: in the ordinary case a convenor is handing a decision to a room they are standing in, which is a different act from giving it away.
- `founding-ceremony` (the surface) — **merged into `setup.html`** (Ed 361, 2026-08-18 evening; the filename redirects). Kept for the reasoning: **document-creation, seen by somebody else** (Ed, 2026-08-18: *the admin’s cards are visible to the roster (but not editable by them); the roster’s cards are visible to the admin (with completion bar)*). One tab group at the head of the document holds the whole constitution — the convenor’s settings and the room’s questions in one strip — and what differs is only which tabs you may act on: a member answers the ones handed over, reads the rest, and watches the ones they have answered fill. That is why almost nothing lives in the file. Also **what a member sees when they arrive** (Ed: *the roster members as they arrive get their opening ceremony questions in the right-sidebar as cards, and once they're done they can start*), so it is session-view with the ceremony's questions where judgments will be, and the charter readable behind them. Three states, switchable in its topbar so all are inspectable: **ask · waiting · settled**. Its parts: `consent-slider`, the control for stating a minimum, with **nothing preselected** — a suggested value is exactly the anchoring blind collection exists to prevent — and greyed until touched, because a range control with no default still paints its thumb somewhere and would otherwise read as answered; `privacy-ladder`, the disclosure choices ordered most-private-first with the rungs *above* your answer dimmed rather than hidden, since *the most I will accept* only reads as a ladder if you can see what you are refusing; and `distribution-strip`, the published shape of what people asked for, without names. **The roster is on the screen while you answer** (Ed, 2026-08-17) — the whole reframing of the surface: what a member is judging is not what number feels right but *how much of these particular people I need behind a change*, and the room is a list of names you either know or you do not. The waiting state shows a count and nothing else: no names, no values, no running maximum, since any of those would let the room read itself before it had finished answering.
- `proposal-rate` — **one decision since Ed’s merge of 2026-08-18** (*this is actually one place where there should be one decision instead of two*): the ⏱️ **Proposal Rate** card, stating itself in his sentence — *Members start with x ✏️ each, up to a maximum of y. They get an additional ✏️ every z minutes. Successful ✏️s are refunded.* Never “the economy” and never “tokens”; the **stake** stays a flat 1 (SPEC §13). The composer carries the three numbers inside the sentence as inputs; the delegated founding question asks the fewest-to-start (most generous answer wins). Supersedes the 🪙/💧 pair. The drip runs on **real minutes everywhere** (Q353 closed, v0.48): %-of-window pacing retired, the sentence's unit is the mechanism's own, and moving the close touches nobody's wallet.
- `wallets` — **every power is an object you hold, kept where you can see it, spent by flying it** (Ed, 2026-08-20; designed, **not built** — Q440–461). The `edit-wallet` was the economy's readout; four notes of one morning turned it into a family, and the family is the stronger idea because it makes every power on the surface the same kind of thing. Four currencies, four scarcity rules, one gesture — **🪶 founding** (four feathers: three spent on the birth's own acts, the fourth permanent), **✒️ drafting** (one, perpetual — a pen is not spent), **✏️ proposing** (many; spent, dripped, refunded on success), **🏛️** the consensus voice (one at a time, returned whole; the verb is still open, Q450). Which says without a word what the copy kept having to explain: **a member's power is limited in quantity and unlimited in scope; the founder's is unlimited in quantity and limited in scope.** Ed's names: **`propose-wallet`** ✏️ (renaming `edit-wallet` — "edit" named the currency after what it buys, where the others name it after the *act*), **`draft-wallet`** ✒️, **`consensus-wallet`** 🏛️, `quill-wallet` 🪶, and `wallet-flight` for the shared gesture, which reuses `arcFrames` (session-view.html:4483) — the same bowed arc, tumble and reduced-motion fallback that already pays for a proposal. Four rules the family carries. **Placement**: 🪶 top left where the logo goes — and once its three founding feathers are spent, *the one that remains is the logo*, which opens a new document, so the mark of docs.vote is the act of making one; ✏️ ✒️ 🏛️ on the right beside `me`. **The hold ladder**: the gravity of an act is the length of its hold — 🪶 a click (*only 🪶 happens at the speed of thought*), ✒️ one second, ✏️ held for its flight, 🏛️ the ten-second `assembly-press`. **Per-setting permission is a property of the lock, never of the wallet**: the pen is in your topbar because you hold it somewhere, and each setting's ✒️ tab says whether it turns *there* — one pen, many locks, exactly as 🏛️ is one object usable only on constitutional questions. And **no power arrives without acknowledgment** (Ed: *we should stick to our rules*) — the unacknowledged-decision rule seen from the other side, since a power you were never asked for is also something now true of you that you did not do: 🏛️ at arrival, ✒️ at the save, ✏️ at 🍾, each grant a task and each animation firing from that press, which makes the pencil-storm **per member on their own clock** rather than one moment broadcast at a room. 🪶 takes no task — *you are conferred your founding 🪶s by navigating to docs.vote*. Grants **stage behind the constitutional OKs** (Ed): you are told what room you are in before you are handed the tools.
- `🍾 Begin` — **the founder's explicit act of starting the document** (Ed, 2026-08-20; designed, not built — Q440). The moment had been happening as a side effect of the last founding question resolving; it is now a task, committed with ✒️, **stating its whole batch on the card**: the founder lays down ✒️ and 🛡️ on the Text, members gain ✏️ on it, judging opens, the constituted-at time is stamped, the threshold ramp anchors, and §9.6a's free hand closes. The only place in the product where the whole of what starting means is written down. The batch is **not offered as a choice on the surface** but the module must permit one (Ed: *let's say no but make provision for that in the code*). Its card carries the **readiness readout** — per-person and per-question, founder-only — because the founder is making a judgement call about people they invited; it **informs and never blocks** (*until 🍾, we should let the founder get on with founding*).
- `the close` — **the clock closes the document; nobody presses anything** (Ed, 2026-08-20, *100%*; designed, not built — SPEC §4.6 v0.62, fully settled by Ed's answers of the same evening). The closing act happened when the close was set, so T=0 is the room's own decision executing: a **final adoption batch** regardless of cooldown phase, constitutional motions resolve kept (the 🏛️ returns), a pending 👑 fails closed (carried-but-unassented, into the backlog — no crown-lapse auto-pass: lapse is absence, the close is everybody's deadline), a mid-typing draft survives unproposable, an outstanding invitation expires, and **every member and invitee is mailed a link to the record**. Its parts: **`session-clock`** — one plain line in the topbar centre saying where the document is in its life (empty before 🍾 and on a perpetual document; the countdown while windowed, at Ed's granularity ladder — days beyond a week, hours inside one, 20-minute steps inside six hours, 10-minute steps inside the hour, never finer, never seconds; "Frozen — 3 must return"; "Closed [date]" forever); clicking it opens ⏰, so the clock is the door to moving the close. **The topbar reads the document · the room · you** (Ed, 466b): left 🪶 and the title, middle pulse · clock · quorum, right your wallets and `me` — and **the approval threshold is not topbar material** (Ed, 466c: an abstract number that means something only beside the decision it prices — it lives in the ✒️ clause, in a judgment card's own copy of what carrying takes now, and on a ✔'s record of the bar it cleared, never as chrome; he'd think differently were it a simple vote threshold). The clock is cold at every distance, the last hours' urgency belonging to the questions, and a ⏳ entry short of its floor saying so in its teaser inside the final 24 hours (a count, never a direction). **`closing-card`** 🥂 — 🍾's mirror (Ed: *we can only assume that participants have been drinking the 🍾 throughout the session*): a news card pinned until OK, one per member on their own clock, stating its whole batch — final as of when, what adopted, what carried, names revealed per the anonymity ladder, record published — and **OK on it signs the document** (Ed, 468c): a closing comment collected by the rationale mechanic is the signing rationale, blank real, dissent as welcome as praise, landing in the closing section's signatures block. The **wallet farewell** fires from that OK — remaining ✏️s and the 🏛️ fly into the card, the pencil storm inverted; the founder's ✒️ leaves the same way. The **closed page** is the stranger's door's opposite: same URL, every clause grey, every tab filed, the `sealed-speaker` in its revealed form, wallets and pulse gone — and the document ends **bracketed**: constitution · charter · **the backlog as ranked document text**, each unresolved question a paragraph wearing the third filed mark (**undecided**, drawn grey in the ✔/✖ family — settled 2026-08-20, Ed: *⏸️ it is! (but draw your own to match ✔️)*: two bars at the tick and cross's stroke width, currentColor), its tab opening its record card, the signatures beneath. The record’s front door is the foot of the document (seeds Q464). Build order when Ed says go: the topbar rework in session-view (clock to the middle, bar stat removed, quorum joining it — measurable, probe-safe), then the drawn PAUSE in cards.js (probe-gated like any cards.js change), then the 🥂 card and the closed-state fixture, which wants a “settle the session” stagehand button in the dev bar the way ⏩ settles the founding.
- `Text` — **there is no "starting text"** (Ed, 2026-08-20; designed, not built — Q440). One setting, the document's text, holding a value and both powers like anything else; "starting" named the moment we were looking at it from, not a property of the setting. This retires SPEC §9.7's claim that the starting text sits outside the holder system and that holding it crowns nobody — a founder who keeps ✒️ or 🛡️ on the Text is a 👑 by the same rule as anywhere else. 🛡️ on the Text means an **adoption** waits on the founder's accept, which is assent over the drafting mechanism itself and the largest single power in the design; Ed keeps it available for consistency.
- `the four verbs` — **🪶 founding · ✒️ drafting · ✏️ proposing · 🏛️ (consensus, wording open)** (Ed, 2026-08-20). Read as a ladder they are **who has to agree**: 🪶 nobody, because nothing exists yet; ✒️ nobody, because you hold the power; ✏️ enough of the room, at the approval threshold; 🏛️ everybody. A complete account of authority in the product in four glyphs. The naming corrected a frame: ✒️ is not "the founder's direct-change power", it is **drafting** — writing the thing — and *proposing* is what you do when you cannot draft, which is the same distinction the wallets draw in objects. **The pre-start blind founding answers are a type of 🏛️** (Ed, Q446c): the founding answer and the constitutional motion are one act in two eras, both blind while they run, both resolving by a rule no minority can be overridden by — maxima before the start, unanimity after. Which is why the verb cannot be *amending* (before the start nothing is amended, only set) and must be about **consensus**.
- `quill line` — **🪶 covers the acts performed before anybody in the world could reach the document** (Ed, 2026-08-20): *solo acts, freely done, with no oversight*. The line falls at **the save**, because that is when the outside world can first knock — a stranger can navigate to the URL, get a real page, and be refused; refused, but engaging. So the pen is issued when the URL goes live, and the two glyphs meet exactly once, with no overlap and no gap. Same hand, different instrument, and the instrument is the marker of when the founder became accountable to somebody.
- `stranger's door` — **there is no login screen** (Ed, 2026-08-20; designed, not built — Q455/456). Logging in is asking for your address and mailing you a link, which is the **📧 task** the founder answers at the birth and every applicant answers on the way in — so a stranger arrives at the three columns like everybody else, the document is there, and their rail holds one card. `liveLogin`'s dashed monospace box was the last surface that was not the session-view. The page is **redacted rather than blank**: the title shows, the document's shape shows as honest grey bars at real metrics (grey because *grey means nothing is being asked of you*, and a redacted paragraph asks nothing — black bars to be built as an experiment and looked at), and **the rules are public while the text is private** — the constitution reads plainly, because a stranger deciding whether to knock needs to know how joining works, which makes the door the page you read before deciding to apply. The holding sentence is Ed's: *The founder [name] is deciding if you can see this document* — and an unnamed founder is **The founder**, not *Anonymous*, which makes §9.0c's Anonymous rule a rule about how a member appears **in the room**. At the birth there is nothing to redact and the line is *"The constitution is being drafted"*. One screen with one changing sentence serves every later state — **who decided, what they decided, what you may do about it** — and a stranger is the **fifth seat** in the dev dropdown. Before 🌍 is settled a document is **members-only**, stated in SPEC and carried on the surface as an interim clause identical for every reader (*Until the members decide, the document is visible to members only*), because the by-deviation grammar would otherwise leave a stranger reading a rule the members cannot see.
- `edit-wallet` — **renamed `propose-wallet` by Ed 2026-08-20** (see `wallets`; the entry below describes it as built). ✏️s drawn as glyphs, one vanishing per use, the next inking in as the drip accrues: a token *is* an edit, so the wallet says what it buys without a word. **Above four it counts rather than draws** (`✏️✏️✏️✏️+5`), because glyphs are only good at *a few* and a longer row is a bar chart nobody asked for in which a single spend is invisible; and the tray now says **when** the next one lands rather than how far off it is, which is the question anyone standing at an empty wallet is actually asking (Ed, 2026-08-17). Since the composer landed it actually moves — Propose spends one, Withdraw returns it. Note Q251 — at v1 defaults members still sit near the cap, and a visibly depleting wallet reintroduces friction the economy does not require.
- `session-view` — the default member surface: the charter, readable top to bottom, with every contested clause marked where it bites and the `needs-you-queue` beside it. Design: design/session-view.html; reasoning in design/session-view.notes.md (deleted 2026-08-23; `git show aba22fd:design/session-view.notes.md`), which opens with the design system of the day. Its zones:
  - `contents-rail` (left) — the charter's own headings, following the reader, adopted sections only. Carries no explanatory note and, since Ed 2026-08-17, **no title either** — nor does the queue: "Amendments" scrolled away with the page while "Contents" stayed put, and the asymmetry was worse than either label was worth. Both columns say what they are by what is in them. Each heading carries the **lifecycle marks** of the questions inside it (innermost ownership, capped at four, keeping whatever is still actionable and dropping ☑️ first); a heading holding exactly one question opens it on click. Scrolls inside itself, scrollbar hidden.
  - `needs-you-queue` (right) — a **margin index**: every entry stands beside its own clause in document order, so *where* an entry sits is never a claim about importance. **Exactly four kinds of entry pin** (Ed, 2026-08-17, narrowing 110): 🔥, an unacknowledged decision, a proposal of your own, and a prioritisation. Every `needs` entry used to, which made the rail a to-do list wearing a margin's clothes — it travelled with you, so wherever you were reading most of what sat beside you was about somewhere else, and the rail's one claim (*this entry stands beside its own clause*) was false for nearly every row on screen. What survives is what is about **you** rather than about the document: the flame is the question you are being asked next, a decision is owed to you and leaves when you press OK, your own proposal carries an act nobody else can perform, and a prioritisation is served rather than found. Everything else stands beside its clause and scrolls with it. Measured on the fixture: 8 pinned of 44, against 20 before. Where several entries stand at **one clause**, they compete in the `tab-stack`'s own order — lifecycle first, then leverage — so whichever decision the gutter would open is the one the rail shows (Ed, 2026-08-17, closing 309 against the pile-in-the-rail option I had raised it with: the rail already knew how to choose, it just had no tiebreak for entries at the same position and was falling through to fixture order). The two columns share one `leverage` comparator rather than two copies of a formula, because an ordering two surfaces do not share is a disagreement waiting to be noticed. What pins is pinned on screen (at their clause while it is visible, piled against the band edge once it is not, always in document order, and the ones that do not fit are simply not shown — the "+n further off in the charter" tally went at Ed's word 2026-08-17, reversing 110's *counted, never silently dropped*: the rail annotates a document you can scroll, so a running count of what is a few inches away was a permanent apology for a limit nobody experiences as one). Judged and filed lines stay with their clauses, scroll away, and step around a pinned card rather than hiding under it. **A filed decision is a full-width line like everything else** (Ed, 2026-08-17, retiring 106’s dot rows): collapsing several into a shared row of dots broke the rail’s one rule, since a row packed up to eight document positions into one position, and the cable then had to land on a 20px dot inside a row that was not beside the clause it pointed at. Space was the argument for the dots, and the rail has since stopped drawing what does not fit, so it was paying for a saving it no longer needs. A filed line is the same object as an unacknowledged one, quieter — same shape, less weight. **Which** of them fit is decided by urgency, most urgent first — a ranking over *admission*, never over position, and never a threshold: nothing is too unimportant to appear, the rail simply runs out of room, and the next ones down surface as the urgent ones are dealt with. The most urgent card (🔥) and whatever is open are exempt from the fit cap, and **the open entry's claim on its clause's line is absolute** — entries that cannot fit around it are dropped into the count rather than displacing it, so its wire reads flat (222). Entries not asking for a judgment cannot be ranked on judgment leverage: an unacknowledged decision takes the middle of the scale, and a `deadlocked` race is ranked instead by its **bounty score** near the top — judgment leverage there is nil precisely because drafting leverage is the highest on the surface (223).
  - `lifecycle mark` — one glyph per entry, the same alphabet in all three columns (contents rail, document gutter, queue): 💡 a proposal, and it wants your judgment · 🔥 wants you most, and its rail entry carries **no rationale while it is following you** (Ed, 2026-08-17, in two passes) — every other entry's teaser is what makes you decide whether this is the one to open, and 🔥 has already been decided for you, so while it is piled against the band edge miles from its clause the teaser answers a question nobody is asking. But that is a fact about being **pinned**, not about being the flame: scroll to its own clause and it stands in the margin beside the text it is about, among neighbours that all carry theirs, and *then it is an ordinary 💡* (Ed) — reading as the odd one out there is the opposite of what dropping the teaser was for. So the teaser is always in the markup and `layoutQueue` hides it for the one case, testing the **anchor's** position rather than the entry's, which is what stops it oscillating: showing a teaser makes the entry taller, and a rule that read the entry's own position could hide what it had just shown. One side effect worth keeping: piled at the edge with the words gone, the `evidence-meter` fills the whole entry, so the flame is also the clearest progress bar in the rail · ⚔️ stuck, wants a draft not a judgment (its action is ✏️) — **and you never see it until you have judged the race yourself** (Ed, 297/298, 2026-08-17): a deadlocked race is an ordinary 💡 until it has nothing left to ask you, which makes deadlock a *personal* state like ⏳ and the alphabet consistent — a mark says what the document wants **from you**, not what state the machine is in. Better evidence too: the judgments that rule extracts are the ones most likely to unstick the race, and a deadlock declared before the room has finished judging is declared early. It is tested before ⏳ everywhere, because it is the state that replaces it; yellow still, because hot is for actions and a bridge is one. ⚔️ rather than ❌ because a cross says *this failed* — nothing failed, two things are still fighting, and that is what makes a bridge worth writing · 🌶️ a salience diagonal, asking which of two questions is hotter (Ed, 2026-08-17, replacing ⚖️: weighing is what *every* card here asks for, so scales said nothing that distinguished this one — and a balance is the wrong figure for a question about heat; it also turns to mush at 13px where a chilli is one silhouette, and it is the hue the card already wears) · ⏳ you have judged, still running · ↻ the ground shifted under a judgment of yours, so **that judgment** is void and the race will ask you again. Not *comes back rebuilt*, which is how it read until 2026-08-17 and which invites the reading that the **proposal** is rebuilt: nothing is rewritten and no new candidate appears, the wordings are the ones that were already there, and what is re-served is a *pair to judge*. Nor is it a retired race — the race is still running, and running harder, since a re-opened one carries a 1.5× routing boost (§6.2). The card is a receipt; the race is the thing with a lifecycle. · **✔ green** decided, charter changed — the one decided state that is *news*, so it takes a light green wash (`--lc-changed`) rather than the grey, and it is the only one that pins itself until acknowledged · **✖ green** decided, incumbent held — a receipt rather than news, since somebody proposed and nobody liked it and the charter is exactly as it was, so it washes grey — and it **pins only if you judged it** (Ed, 2026-08-17), because then it is not news but it is an *answer you are owed*, and a decision you put something into must not simply vanish. A retired race you never touched goes straight to a filed dot. The rule underneath: **a decision announces itself if it changed the document, or if you are part of why it did not** · **✔ grey / ✖ grey** filed, and which way it went. **The four decided marks are drawn glyphs rather than emoji** (Ed, 2026-08-17): ✅❎☑️🔄 were the only marks in the alphabet that came as a coloured plate with a shape knocked out of it, so beside the silhouettes they read as a different *kind* of object rather than a different state. As text glyphs (U+FE0E) they take a colour, so the palette chooses it rather than the reader emoji font, and one function draws them so the four columns cannot drift. The tick and cross are **two SVG paths on one stroke width**, not characters (Ed, 2026-08-17: *I still see the calligraphic check*). U+2714 was the right code point and the wrong object: system-ui has no glyph for it, so it falls through to Segoe UI Symbol where the heavy check mark is a tapered brush stroke — solid at 34px in a test, a thin squiggle at 18px in a gutter chip beside a geometric cross, and a different mismatch on every machine. Drawn, they cannot drift, they scale, and they take `currentColor` so the lifecycle classes still colour them. ↻ stays a character: it has no partner whose weight it must equal. **Filed keeps which way it went** (Ed, 2026-08-17): ☑️ collapsed both outcomes the moment you acknowledged them, throwing away the only thing anybody wants from a settled clause in a margin — *did this change or not*. Same glyphs, grey, because the difference between decided and filed is whether it still wants something from you. Everything in the code keys on the mark **kind** rather than the character, since two states now share one. 🔥 means *an ordinary judgment that wants you most*, so ⚔️ and 🌶️ are never it. **✏️ means *you wrote this*** — the writing action on a button, and the mark on anything of your own from the first keystroke to the seal, proposed or not (Ed, 260: it stays ✏️ rather than becoming 💡 once proposed, because the contents rail draws marks with no colour and green is what would otherwise have carried the difference). Renamed 2026-08-16 (Ed, 241): a rail entry is somebody's proposal rather than a question the system invented, and promoting the action to its own glyph puts each nearer its job — a bulb is about having had an idea, a pencil is about writing.
  - `yours` — the fifth lifecycle state (Ed, 2026-08-16): a proposal **of your own**, green, from the first keystroke to the moment it seals. It sits outside the judge's ladder, because nothing is asked of you — SPEC §3.3 counts your preference for your own candidate without asking — and it is not settled either, because you can always act on it. ✏️ throughout. Drawn as a **full card while it is an unproposed draft**, so you can read your rationale back as you type it, with a flat wash and **no fill** (there is nothing yet to be close to); **one line once proposed**, with the fill arriving as the completion bar. Never crowded off the screen: it is force-kept beside the flame and the open card, because the rail admits by *judgment* leverage and a proposal of yours scores nothing there while carrying the largest remaining act — withdraw.
  - `salience-diagonal` — the card asking which of two open **questions** deserves more of the room's attention. **Not a rate** (SPEC §8.3a, Ed 2026-08-17, replacing the flat ~1-in-10): prioritisation is only worth attention when there is genuinely too much to do, so a diagonal is served only when the document holds at least **E live questions** — as many open disputes as there are people on the roster, counting a race **once** however many candidates it holds, since they all prioritise to the same place — and then **only to a participant with nothing else to judge**, because everyone else's time is better spent judging. **Served, not offered** (Ed, 2026-08-17): when the queue empties the card simply arrives. Asking first needs somewhere to put the question — the only place was the top of the rail, which is a margin index and not a place for a banner — and the answer was never in doubt. Until it is served the diagonal is nowhere on the surface at all: not in the rail, not in the gutter. It also carries **no progress bar**, because salience has no threshold to clear and no quorum to ratify (§8.3a), so a fill on it would claim a finish line that does not exist; its rail entry washes flat. It terminates: served only while a pair would still move the salience ranking, hard ceiling of three in a row, then *you are up to date*. Known cost, written into the spec rather than papered over: salience is then measured on the most active members. Tolerable because the ranking is advisory and never touches adoption. A patch turned inside out: one judgment, two anchors in different parts of the charter, two queue entries joined by a wire spine. Since Ed, 276 it is the **same card as everything else**: its head is the one on the surface that is not a clause (a diagonal has none — it spans two), so it holds the question being put; its field is the two questions, each with its name, its description, the clause it is about quoted underneath, and a *Prefer this*. No `sealed-speaker` on either — the line under each question describes the dispute rather than arguing for it, and drawing a person there would claim an author it does not have. Choosing ranks the questions and never touches either text. It swallows only the clause it stands in, so the far one is quoted while its paragraph is still in the document — accepted by Ed (277). It takes **one** rail entry, not one per clause, standing at the earlier of its two and titled *Prioritise A vs B* (Ed, 2026-08-17), set over four lines — `Prioritise:` / the first name / `vs` / the second (Ed, 284), because on one line the two names collided and truncated, which is the one thing an entry made for weighing them against each other must never do: a patch stands at several places because that is where its work is, but neither clause is where a diagonal lives, so standing at both said "there is something here" twice about a card that is really about the pair.
  - `suggestion-anchor` — **the prose carries no highlight** (Ed, 2026-08-17). A challenged clause used to wear its queue card's lifecycle hue behind the words; the whole of that colour now lives in the `clause-tab` beside it, so the charter reads as a charter and the margin does the work. The geometry stays — `.anch` and the `clause-head`'s block keep their padding and negative margin, because the card-opening motion is measured against the two boxes being identical — only the paint is gone. A held-open gap keeps its wash: there is no text there to leave unmarked, so the colour *is* the thing. Since 2026-08-16 a clause is **not** a button: clicking it puts a caret in it (`always-on-typing`), and the gutter mark is what opens the decision card. `insert-anchor` is a blank held-open gap with 💡 beside it. `chip-gutter` is the left margin they stack in, keeping the prose column unbroken.
  - `clause-tab` — the object in the `chip-gutter`: one per live or filed decision at that clause, carrying its `lifecycle mark`, and the **only** way into a decision card from the document. **An object, not a glyph with a hit area** (Ed, 2026-08-17): a resting ground in its own lifecycle hue, the contact shadow, 34×30 with the glyph above document size, lifting on hover and going *down* on press — the same object the rail's sealed dot already was, since they are one control in two columns. **On an open card they are literally its tabs** (Ed, 2026-08-17): they already travelled into the head, already stood beside the card, and clicking another already switched the card to it — what was missing was that any of it looked like a tab strip. **The strip does not reorder** (Ed, 2026-08-17): the active card's tab used to be lifted to the top, so every switch dealt the column again and the tab you were aiming at moved out from under the pointer on arrival — a tab strip is a fixed set of places you move a highlight around, which is the whole of what makes it a strip rather than a list of shortcuts. It costs nothing that the active one is no longer first, because the gutter pile only ever opens its *front* tab, which is index 0 in the same stack order the strip uses, so the mark you clicked is still at index 0 when the card arrives. The active tab instead **grows 8px out to the left** (Ed, 2026-08-17) — leftward is the only direction available, since the column is right-aligned onto the card's edge, so growing is automatically growing left and the strip keeps its single vertical joint; the 8px goes on `padding-left` as well as `width` so the glyph does not move. Rounded on the left only so the right edge is a joint; the active one deepened and 6px wider so it carries under the card's left edge with no seam, casting leftward only. The extra width is pushed out with a negative right margin, because the column is right-aligned and widening alone moves the glyph — the one thing the card-opening motion is built on it not doing. It **keeps its colour when active** rather than taking the card's white: now that the prose carries no highlight the gutter holds the only lifecycle colour on that side of the screen, and a tab that went white on opening would throw away the one thing it is there to say. A flatter glyph set is Q288.
  - `tab-stack` — what a clause's tabs are when the card is **closed** (Ed, 2026-08-17). A strip of tabs seen closed is a *pile*: the front tab is the object, the rest slide up under it leaving a sliver each, and opening it expands the pile into the tab strip down the side of the card, which already existed and already worked because a card is ~380px tall. It replaces a column that ran at full height — § Bringing a Guest was 129px of tabs beside a 36px clause, so three of four marks stood beside prose they had nothing to do with and the overhang rendered *on top of* the held-open gap's own mark below. Four rules. **The front is what most wants you**, and that order is deliberately *not* `KEEP_ORDER`: the contents rail ranks by what must not be lost (so ✏️ sits above 💡, since a proposal of yours carries the largest remaining act), the stack ranks by what most wants you (so ✏️ sits *below* 💡, since your own work is asking nothing and you do not click into a pile to be shown it). Retention and priority are different questions with the same-looking answer, and the stack is the first place they disagree. **The slivers carry hue, not a count** — colour is the whole vocabulary, so an orange sliver says *something here is urgent* without drawing a flame, and a count would be the tally Ed removed from the queue the same morning; they are mixed stronger than a tab face (42% against 18%) because four pixels of an 18% mix is a smudge, and squared at the top because a radius eats a 4px band. **The slivers are inert**: one stack, one target — the full strip is a click away, and a 4px sliver is not a control to ask anybody to hit. **The pile is fitted, not fixed** — `fitStacks()` runs after layout over the whole gutter and shrinks each stack's peek until it reaches no further than the next mark, which is what makes two anchors claiming one strip *structurally* impossible rather than merely rarer; it is the same move the `needs-you-queue` makes when it steps entries around a pinned card, and it is the answer to the question 294 could not reach, since each `chipcol` is laid out inside its own anchor and knows nothing of the one below. The residual — a single tab is 30px and cannot shrink — is Q308. **Filed decisions live at the bottom of the strip as a pile of their own** (Ed, 2026-08-17, answering 294): a card lines its live tabs up at full height and the ones already filed keep the closed posture underneath them, newest at the top — because only the top glyph shows when it is shut, so it must be the last thing that happened here — until you click the pile, when they line up too and **the card grows to hold them all**, a floor on its height rather than a height. Which is the stack applied one level down, and it is what makes the answer to *where does history live* cost nothing: no history tab, no separate surface, no rule about when a filed mark may appear. Filed decisions stay **off the closed gutter**, where the pile is still live-only. Decided-but-unread is not filed and stays with the live tabs, because it is still asking for its OK. Related fix: hole rectangles in the wire-shadow clip are now **merged** before being punched, because the clip is one `evenodd` path and two overlapping holes XOR back to solid; a pile overlaps by 26px of every 30, and 74 boxes in the fixture resolve to 48 non-overlapping holes.
  - `decision card` — **the clause, opened.** It *replaces* its paragraph rather than opening beneath it (Ed, QA 2026-08-16; also settles 265), so the thing being proposed about appears exactly once on screen. The shape is a post and its replies: `clause-head` → `proposal-block` for each candidate → commit row, with a hairline between each of the three bands. One card for every type, differing only in what fills the two halves — quick card: clause + one proposal; race: clause + two; patch: the same per site, plus the ↑/↓ stepper; sealed record: clause + the whole field ranked with its numbers. Submitting does not close the card. **Opening**: the card does not grow from nothing — it grows from exactly the height at which only its `clause-head` is showing, so frame one looks like the document with the paragraph still in it, the gap then slides open beneath the clause, and the field fades in 60ms behind the motion. The clause itself never moves (measured 0px on every card type). **Closing runs the card's whole box back onto its paragraph** (Ed, 2026-08-17) — height down to the `clause-head`'s own washed block, padding away, eyebrow shut, lift and white ground gone, and the top edge descending by exactly what it stops holding so the clause still does not move. It used to stop at head height, which is where *opening* starts but is not a paragraph, so the last 93px arrived as a jump; opening gets away with that discrepancy because it happens in the frame of the click, closing put it after the motion, which is the one order the eye will not forgive. The card's own mark closes it — a stale guard had made every mark inside a card inert.
    - `clause-head` — the clause itself at the top of the card, at document size and colour, under an eyebrow. **It keeps the clause's own highlight and its own gutter mark** (Ed, 2026-08-17): the washed block inside it is given the same box as a `.anch` paragraph — same negative margin, same padding — so the wash lands on the same rectangle and the `.chipcol` inside it lands in the same gutter column. Measured: the mark you clicked moves 0px in both axes, and the clause text sits on the prose's own axis to the pixel, which is what makes the card read as the clause opening rather than as something replacing it. The card's mark is the same control it was in the gutter, so it also closes the card; other live suggestions at that clause stack under it exactly as they did beside the paragraph. It carries a `lane-bar` exactly when the judgment can keep the clause: on a quick card or a patch the incumbent is one of the two things being judged; on a **race** both candidates are challengers and nothing on the card can vote to keep it (the adoption-threshold decides that, SPEC §5), so there the head is reference only.
    - `proposal-block` — one candidate: its wording, stating its own change against the clause (see `result-only`); then its rationale behind a `sealed-speaker`; then its `lane-bar`. The reply in the post-and-replies shape, and the reason each argument now sits with its own wording instead of being mapped column-to-column.
    - `sealed-speaker` — the featureless head-and-shoulders disc a rationale sits behind. Ed's note was that nothing said a *person* was saying the rationale; the disc says it, and its blankness says the rest, because authorship is sealed until the record (SPEC §3.4). Drawn rather than left as a plain circle — a bare disc reads as a bullet, and a bullet says *list item*.
    - `result-only` — a proposal states **the text as it would stand**, with what it adds marked green and nothing struck through (Ed, 274, 2026-08-17: *normal people struggle to read or interpret redlines*). This restores 91 and retires the `redline` that briefly replaced it. One rendering everywhere — card, lane and record — so a proposal reads the same wherever you meet it. Two rules survive from the redline: a **marking floor**, below half the new text surviving from the clause the proposal states itself plainly (which is what keeps a race candidate, usually a whole rewrite, from being lit green end to end — Q92); and **punctuation as its own token**, without which a clause that gains a comma marks the whole word. The blind spot it accepts: in a stacked card a proposal whose only change is a *partial* cut looks like the clause above it, and you find it by reading. The worst case is covered — a proposal that removes the text **entirely** would render as blank, which cannot be told from unchanged, so it says *(all text removed)* instead, drawn as a pseudo-element so it is never content the caret can land behind or `htmlToMd` can serialise.
    - `lane-bar` — the control strip at the foot of each text, replacing "the lanes are the buttons" (197, retired at Ed's QA: clicking a clause is what `always-on-typing` means by *put a caret here*, so the gesture could not also mean *I prefer this*). A radio marked **Prefer this** / **Preferred** (272), plus ✏️ *edit this*. The rule that governs it: **a lane may carry what is about that lane, and nothing else**. The radio is the **loudest thing in the lane** (Ed, 286) — UI size at weight 600 on a 1.5px edge with a 15px dot, above the ✏️ beside it — because since 197 was retired the radio *is* the judgment, and a card whispering the one act it exists for was the wrong way round.
    - `commit row` — the card's bottom band, and the third radio (Ed, 2026-08-16, reversing 202/204/209/213). **Indifferent** sits at the left as the last of the trio — labelled, not drawn as 🤷, because every candidate carries a radio now and *neither of these* is plainly one more answer to the same question; it stays out of the lanes because it is a judgment about the *pair* (SPEC §3.2). The green **✓** sits at the right, always present and **greyed until something is chosen** — an absent button gave the row no shape and the reader no idea what finishing looked like. **❄️ appears only on the 🔥 card**, immediately left of the ✓ and paired with it (Ed, 2026-08-17). It is not an act on the judgment — nothing is skipped, recirculated or decayed and no evidence is touched — but a **toggle on the flame**: it takes the card out of the running for 🔥, so it drops back to an ordinary 💡 and the next most urgent question takes the flame. That is what a member means when they want to move on, and it is reversible because a mood is. Pressing it **closes the card** (going somewhere else is the whole gesture); un-pressing it does not, because there you are coming back. Its pressed state is the toggle, so it needs no words — the old control needed five. 🤷 has left the surface. **Every radio on a card lines up down its left edge** (Ed, 2026-08-17), locked cards included — a locked one used to keep a centred row on the argument that a lone disabled radio pushed left leaves the other end empty, which is true and does not matter: the alignment is what says the three are answers to one question.
  - `deadlock-card` — what a ⚔️ race opens into, and **its own card rather than a race card with a bridge button on it** (Ed, 2026-08-17). By the time you see it the race has nothing left to ask you (SPEC §8.3b), so there is no live judgment card here for it to replace. The clause at the head, reference only; then the card's one raised voice, in the band the field would otherwise start — *the room has stopped moving on this; more judging will not decide it* — which on any other card would be a caveat at the foot and here is the point; then **everything in flight**, full width, oldest first, because arrival order is the only ordering that is not a ranking and a field of eight is largely a conversation in which each wording answers the ones before it; then what you said as a grey record line, one line for however many pairs it took, since the card is about the race and not about any pair in it; then **the desk**: the same `laneBoxHtml` the `editing-card` uses, with the same commit row — 🗑️ at the very left, hold-✏️ at the very right — because the eight wordings are not a reference you go away from, they are what you write *against*, so the reading room and the desk are one surface (Ed, 2026-08-17). Before a draft exists the lane holds the clause and is a real editor, and the first keystroke opens the draft with that character applied — `always-on-typing` applied to a box instead of a paragraph, and literally the same function underneath. Every wording in the field carries ✏️ *propose edit*, the lane bar minus its radio, so you take whichever came closest and write from it. The field is marked `result-only` **with the floor forced off**: left alone the floor marks three of eight, because each is far enough from the clause to count as a rewrite, so the card that most needs the marking is the one the floor silences — and the floor exists to stop a *lane* being lit beside its incumbent, where here the incumbent is at the head and comparison is the point. **No standings, and therefore no lock** (Ed, 303): a card showing rankings would be the briefing §3.5 permits on a race out of the judgment stream, and would have to close your judgments with it, because reading the standings and then revising is informed judgment in a blind field. A card showing only wordings and rationales leaks nothing — both are already public — so it takes nothing away and needs no rule. Two renderings tried and rejected, both recorded in Q301: marking against the clause (`result-only` marks three of eight, since at this distance they are rewrites and the floor states them plain) and ranking (unavailable by construction — being level is what deadlock means). The open question is that at eight the comparison you need is each-against-the-others, which nothing computed against the incumbent can draw (Q305, Q306).
  - `sealed record` — every entry opens one, filed ones included. **The card starts at the top of the field** (Ed, 2026-08-17): on a decided card the top of the ranking *is* the clause — the winner where it carried, the incumbent where it held — so the top entry **is** the head, keeping the head machinery (the gutter mark, the washed block on the paragraph axis, the opening geometry) and giving up only its label, which becomes its own rank, outcome and score. It carries no box either: a green-bordered panel round the winner was the fourth device saying *this one*. Then **one list of the whole field**, winner first, each proposal full width with its win probability as a **percentage**, right-aligned into a column. **The incumbent is in the list** (Ed, 2026-08-17), and at a true place: every score is the probability that proposal beats the current text, so the current text sits at **50%** by construction — which makes the column say *these two beat what we had and those three did not*, the most useful sentence on the card and one that was nowhere on it before. It wears a dashed edge, shows its own 50% at a muted weight (a construction, not a measurement), and is labelled *the text it replaced* or *the text that stood*. **Whichever text is the clause at the head does not print itself again** — the winner where it carried, the incumbent where it held — which is the whole of the duplication three separate bands used to produce. The bar is stated **once**, in the field label, rather than once per proposal and again in the record. Plus quorum met and what you did. Unread decisions pin themselves until you press **OK**, at the bottom right where every card commits; reading alone does not clear it. The button is a word, not a glyph: a tray-and-arrow reads as *download*, an archive box says *storage*, an eye says *seen* rather than *done* — there is no glyph in common use for *I have taken this in*, and when a set has no member the word is the answer rather than the fallback.
  - `evidence-meter` — closeness-to-resolution as a magnitude, never a direction. Lives **only** on queue cards, where the wash's fill *is* the bar; decision cards carry no progress. Since Ed, 285 the bar sits on a **ground** of the same hue across the whole card, so the card *is* its colour and the bar says how far through that colour you are — a card had no visible right edge before, and width is the one dimension of a rail entry that carries no meaning and so has no excuse for being the one you cannot see. The ground is derived from the bar's own colour by swapping its alpha, so the two can never drift apart; and the bar's leading edge is drawn a second time in that colour, because at the bottom of the urgency scale the fill would otherwise be too close to its own ground to show an edge, and the edge is the whole of what a progress bar says.
  - `queue-wire` — for the open judgment only, the run from its entry down the gutter into the place in the charter it refers to. **A patch is joined at its cards, not in the rail** (Ed, 2026-08-17): the spine down the middle of the gutter is gone, and the patch's decision cards are linked instead by a cable in the `chip-gutter` that leaves each `clause-tab` by its **left side**, curves onto a run just outside the column, and curves back into the next. Joining the pointers said *these three entries are one*; the thing that is actually one is the three clauses. The side exit is also what retires the stacking problem outright — a cable that never enters the column cannot cross a tab in it — and it lets the cable leave the patch's own tab rather than whatever strip happens to hold it. It carries no cap: a landing ball says *this clause, here*, and both ends of this one are a tab that has already said it. Consecutive segments share the run and overlap at each middle tab, which costs nothing (the ink group carries the alpha) and gives the middle sites a drop off a through-run rather than two cables meeting end to end. `clause-tab`s are punched out of the shadow for the same reason cards are: a tab is an object at card height, and it sits *outside* its card's box. **6px, opaque, and exactly the colour of its own queue card** (Ed, 287 and the QA of 2026-08-17), with a 10px cap at the **document end only** — the rail-end cap said nothing the card it was leaving did not already say, where the far end is the wire's actual claim, *this clause, here*. The colour is the card's wash **composited over white and painted on a white cable of the same weight**: opaque as an object, identical as a colour, which is why it also carries urgency for free — a 🔥 card's cable is the strongest on the surface. Three versions to get there and the last two are one lesson: a hue darkened toward the ink reads as a different object from the card, and the wash used *as a wash* is the right colour in the wrong medium, because a translucent cable shows the charter through it. **Match what the eye sees, not what the token says.** It is **lifted to the cards' own height** (Ed, 289a) — the same three-layer light as `--shadow-xl`, on the container so the union of underlay and colour casts one shadow rather than two of the same cable stacked. A cord lying flat between two things visibly off the page was the odd object out, and depth is the only thing this surface uses to say where something is. The colour layer is painted at **full strength inside a group carrying the alpha**, so every run, cap and link composites exactly once: painting translucent shapes over each other doubled the alpha wherever they met, which made the landing ball darker than its cable and a patch's shared segments darker where they met. 198's one-colour rule was about *kind*, which the wire was never asked to say; lifecycle is what its mark and its wash already say, so the wire agreeing adds no vocabulary. The accent is not missed: only the open judgment ever draws a wire, so its existence already says *this is the open one*. A sealed dot carries no wash, so its wire takes the fixed grey the dot itself wears. It **follows its card's colour**, fading over the same 700ms by the same technique the washes use — born wearing the previous colour, handed the new one after a forced reflow, keyed by the judgment so a redraw at a new scroll position does not re-run the fade (Ed, 2026-08-17). Its shadow is drawn by hand and clipped so that **every** card on the surface is punched out of it, not only the two it joins: the rail is a layer of cards at one height and a cable at that height passes several of them, and a thing does not shadow its own layer. Two nested groups, clip on the outer and the shadow's offset on the inner — a `clip-path` resolves *after* the referencing element's own transform, so both on one group moved the holes down with the shadow and left a 3px sliver along every card's top edge. `section-toggle` — the fold triangle, mirrored in the contents rail and the document gutter. The `contents-rail`'s own hierarchy was **inverted** until the housekeeping pass of 2026-08-17 — a Part at 12px, a chapter inside it at 13px, a section inside that at 12.48px, so the outermost level was the smallest text in the column — and it now reads as a divider and two levels: `--t-cap` upper-case bold for a Part, then one size (`--t-small`) for both levels under it, told apart by weight and indent, which is the job indentation was already doing.
- `race-card` — the judging surface: contested text, two candidates, A/B/indifferent/propose-C. In the UI it is the session-view's escalation state. **design/race-card.html is stale and should not be copied from** — it predates the whole current card grammar (yellow cards, per-candidate verdict buttons, "Option A/B" labels), all of which session-view has since replaced. Session-view is the only current word on card design; bringing race-card.html forward is now a rebuild, and Q70 asks whether it should exist at all.
- `composer` — **not a surface**: you compose by editing the charter (Ed, 224–241; built into session-view 2026-08-16). Its parts:
  - `always-on-typing` — every clause **and every heading** carries a caret (headings since Ed, 2026-08-17 — a section that cannot be renamed is one the charter cannot revise), and every input into one is intercepted: the charter is never edited in place, and the keystroke instead opens the `editing-card` with that character already applied. A selection may **span blocks**: the run is flattened to one string with a newline between blocks, the selection is located in *that* string, and the edit applied to it — so deleting a whole section is one edit to one piece of text, which is why it comes out as **one candidate and not a patch** (Ed, 2026-08-17). The block type travels with each origin, so a heading still reads as a heading in the lane. The caret is the accent blue, and **where it lands is announced when the surface moved it** — a short flash that shrinks onto the bar and fades — which fires on the one caret move the reader did not make: typing in the charter carries the caret off into the proposing lane of a card that has just appeared. Not on a click; a click is you putting the caret somewhere and you already know where (Ed, 2026-08-17). Its shape is not settable — CSS gives `caret-color` and nothing else (Q281). This is what makes a caret in a constitutional document safe. It also makes the **gutter mark the only way** into a decision card from the document, which is why the mark is now a 24×22 target rather than a bare glyph (Ed, 224).
  - `editing-card` — the same `decision card` shape as everything else (Ed, 2026-08-16, closing Q275 and Q270 with it): the clause at the head, your draft as the single reply in the one box left on the surface, your rationale a field in the `sealed-speaker`'s own slot — **inside the same lifted box as the wording** (Ed, 2026-08-17): the two are one *editing surface*, because you are expected to fill in both, so they sit at one height with a hairline between rather than as two boxes at different depths — so you write the sentence in the place, and at the weight, everybody else will read it in. Propose and Cancel below. It had been two side-by-side lanes on the argument that while writing you want the original level with what you are typing; that does not survive contact — the original is one line up, your additions are **marked green as you type** (Ed, 263), and a full-width lane is a far better place to write prose than a 300px column. Free to open; the edit is spent at **Propose**, which is where the price is said in words. The rationale's placeholder reads *We should change this because…* and is drawn **out of the text flow** — as inline content it sat in the line and put the caret after it, so an empty field looked like text you could not delete (Ed, 2026-08-17). The lane preserves spaces: it rewrites its own markup on every keystroke so the green marking can follow the caret, and a trailing space collapsed to nothing in the re-render until the blocks were set `pre-wrap`.
  - `lane-controls` — the only editing controls on the surface, in the top right of the `editing-card`'s box (Ed, 2026-08-17). **B**, *I*, and a two-segment **Rich / Markdown** toggle. They were going to live in the topbar; that is the wrong home, because nothing outside an open card can be edited, so a topbar toolbar would be greyed for the whole session except the minute you are writing. A candidate's text **is** markdown — most people edit it rendered, some switch to monospace source to check the characters are exactly what they meant, and that only means anything if the characters are what is stored. Rich mode serialises back through `htmlToMd` so editing rendered never silently drops the emphasis it is showing; switching view **converts the caret offset**, because markdown mode counts the syntax characters and rich mode does not. Inline only — bold, italic, code: a charter is prose, and block structure is already carried by the run of clauses.
  - `draft-site` — a **run of adjacent blocks** — clauses, headings, or both — replaced by one piece of text. One site is **one candidate**, whatever the run's length: a patch is one judgment shown at several *separate* places, and a run is one place that happens to be several blocks long (Ed, 2026-08-17). Editing across a paragraph break joins them (Ed, 225); editing somewhere else makes a second site, and the draft becomes a patch in the making — a card at each place, one Propose, the entries cabled on the wire spine (Ed, 232, 237). One draft at a time, because there is one caret (Q266).
  - `propose-edit` — ✏️ on either lane of a decision card: start writing from *that* wording rather than from the charter's (Ed, 228). `Propose something else` survives only on a **deadlocked** race, where a bridge is the ask rather than an extra.
  - `proposal-row` — **the proposal's whole lifecycle is one commit row** (Ed, 2026-08-17), and the row does not move as the proposal advances: 🗑️ at the **very left** throughout, the commit control at the **very right**. While it is a draft the right slot is a bare **✏️ you hold down**, and while you hold it one of your pencils **flies out of the `edit-wallet` and lands on the button** — the proposal goes in when it arrives, and letting go early sends it home with nothing spent (Ed, 2026-08-17). The gesture and the price are one object: what you are holding down for is the time it takes the edit to travel. Under reduced motion it fades at the wallet and arrives at the button instead — same gesture, same duration, no flight. 🗑️ discards, and nothing has been spent; once pressed the card becomes your-proposal, the right slot becomes a pressed *✏️ Submitted*, and 🗑️ is `withdraw`, which hands the edit back in full (SPEC §3.3a) and is the reason your entry stays pinned. **Withdrawing flies the pencil home** (Ed, 2026-08-17) — the same object that paid, making the return journey in 640ms, leaving in the pose it arrived in and landing flat. Discarding an unproposed draft flies nothing, which is the point of the symmetry: nothing was spent. **The flight is an arc, drawn fresh each time** — a quadratic bowed off the straight run by a signed amount, sampled into keyframes because a shape that changes every flight cannot be a CSS rule; which side it swings and how far are the whole of the variation. Let go and it comes home along its own arc, run backwards at four times the speed. In both directions the **edit in flight is drawn exactly once**: outbound the wallet keeps its count and leaves the traveller's slot empty (it is not spent until it lands), inbound it holds its old count until the pencil is in it — render state, not a poke at the DOM, because the drip rebuilds the wallet every second and was putting the flying pencil straight back. The right-hand control going from the act to the fact of it is exactly what the judgment row's ✓ does when pressed — one lifecycle, not two screens. 🗑️ stays an ordinary outline button rather than a red one: discarding and withdrawing are both real things to do and not dangers. The Propose button is **blue, not green**, a consequence of the palette move rather than a separate decision — green means *decided* and a submission is the start of a decision, and everything else about your work is the accent.
  - Superseded with it: design/composer.html's `drafting-desk` and `arrival-bar`. `the-briefing` (heat-panel, standings-panel, camp-map, why-digest, graveyard, bridge-bar) and `dominated-account` survive but are **not yet rebuilt** — the briefing as an escalation state unrolling above the lanes, and SPEC §3.5 says it may only appear where there is no live judgment left to contaminate, so a cold-start edit gets no briefing at all.
- `motion` — a proposal to change a **setting** rather than a clause (Ed, 2026-08-18), and the one act by which a settled document changes its own rules. Which route it takes is a fact about the setting and not about the motion: an **ordinary** motion is an ordinary race, judged pairwise against the value as it stands and carrying at the bar with quorum — **and since Q390 (Ed, 2026-08-19, SPEC v0.53) it is the race machinery whole**: rival values join the same race (propose-C over values), entries stake and refund like text proposals, and a standing-value change mid-race is a ground shift; identical values dedup by equality; a **constitutional** one is **a unanimous vote on the proposed amendment** (Ed, 2026-08-18, v0.47 — superseding the re-ask-the-founding-question design): accept it or keep what stands, one refusal keeps what stands, blind while it runs. There is therefore **no new object on the surface for amending a constitution**: an ordinary motion is a decision card with a value where the prose would be, and a constitutional motion is the card that asked the question in the first place, live again. An invitation is a constitutional motion (Ed: *drafters can also make proposals to invite new members, which are passed in the constitutional way*), so one member who says no keeps a joiner out. SPEC §9.6; raising one is free and open to any member, limited by the holder rather than the setting — **one 🏛️ out per member at a time**, returned whole on withdrawal or settlement (Ed, 2026-08-18, closing Q327). **It settles live** (v0.48): accept / keep / abstain, abstention never blocks, and it carries the moment every *currently active* member stands at accept-or-abstain with zero keep — no electorate snapshot, the base re-checked on every answer and roster event. **The mover stands at accept from the open** (Ed, 2026-08-18, v0.49: proposers prefer their own proposals, as §3.3 counts an author's own preference without asking — revisable, and 🗑️ stays their way out), and the consent card offers all three answers, the third labelled **Abstain** (Q370 closed (a), Ed's word). And **a carried amendment binds races in flight** (Q328 closed: races always run under the constitution as it stands; past adoptions keep their recorded bar; incoherence is a ground shift).
- `assembly-press` — how a constitutional motion is put (Ed, 2026-08-18): a **full ten-second hold** on the 🏛️ button, deliberately sillier than the flying pencil — the members’ avatars convene one by one into a circle around it (*it’s an assembly!*), so **the assembly is the progress meter**, complete exactly when the motion goes in; release early and it disperses, nothing sent. The composer’s commit flips between Propose (a click) and the hold as the typed value changes route (329a). Pairs with the one-🏛️-per-member limit; under reduced motion the seats appear without the spring but the stagger stays, because the ring is the meter.
- `gazette` — public feed of resolved outcomes; the chamber view is its ambient rendering.
- `bounty score` — resolvable disagreement × salience, the leverage a good draft would have on a deadlocked race. **Retired with it: the bounty-board**, the public tab those races were going to be listed on (Ed, 2026-08-17 — *I don't think we need the bounty board anymore; the sidebar-queue does its job*). The score survives and does the ranking it always did, in the `needs-you-queue`: a ⚔️ race stands near the top of your own margin, beside the clause it is about, which is a better place for it than a tab you have to remember to visit.
- `record-builder` — closing publication: final text + the record (rankings, camps, graveyard, care map, minority map, backlog, audit log).
- `spectator-api` — strictly-public engine-core projection (gazette, live candidates, deadlocked races, document): the only surface spectator views may consume (Q42, backlog).
- `spectator-commentary` — optional LLM commentating view for convention spectators, fed exclusively by the spectator-api; unlike the sim's omniscient `commentator`, it sees no private data (Q42, backlog).

**Tooling:**
- `server` — `packages/server` (`@draft/server`, Q368, 2026-08-19): the thin product host. node:http, no framework; one hash-chained JSONL log per document (loading is `replay` — the log is the only persistence, and it holds answers in plaintext, so the data dir is as sensitive as the room); magic-link auth (creation *is* the §9.7a mail; an invitation link arrives you, §9.6a; revival is logging in); stateless HMAC cookies; a command whitelist that injects the authenticated actor (a body never names who acts) with `view()` as the only member read;
